L1114: 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L1115: 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L1116: 13.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L1117: The Applicant hypothesizes that elamipretide penetrates cell membranes, localizes to the inner mitochondrial membrane, ameliorates electron transport chain deficiencies, increases ATP production in dysfunctional mitochondria, improves mitochondrial morphology, decreases reactive oxygen species, and thereby improves cellular bioenergetics and reduces pathological apoptosis/necrosis.
L1118: The proposed mechanisms related to potential effectiveness of elamipretide for the treatment of BTHS were assessed based on nonclinical information submitted by the Applicant and available published literature. The nonclinical assessment focused on the plausibility that elamipretide has access to the inner mitochondrial membrane and improves mitochondrial function, as assessed using both in vitro and in vivo models relevant to BTHS, as well as other non-BTHS models.
L1119: In Vitro Studies Related to Binding and the Purported Site of Action
L1120: Published literature indicates that elamipretide distributes to cardiomyocytes and other cell types including renal endothelial cells, macrophages, and neurons (Szeto 2014). Studies using model membranes showed that elamipretide binds to CL, tetraoleoyl cardiolipin, and MLCL (Mitchell et al. 2020).
L1121: Uptake of elamipretide (labeled as SS-31) was studied in vitro using N2a cells (a mouse neuroblastoma cell line) and isolated mitochondria (Zhao et al. 2005). Cells were incubated with radiolabeled elamipretide at 37°C for 60 min. Radioactivity was measured in the cell lysate and media. Elamipretide was readily absorbed by the inner mitochondrial membrane, reaching steady state within 30 minutes. Based on the cell volume, the authors estimated that intracellular concentration of elamipretide at steady state was 6-times higher than the extracellular concentration.
L1122: Uptake of radiolabeled elamipretide into mitochondria was also evaluated in mitochondria isolated from mouse liver and rat brain. The maximal rate of uptake occurred within 2 minutes and steady state was reached within 30 minutes. No saturation of absorption was noted up to highest concentration tested (10µM). Based on the mitochondrial volume, the authors estimated that elamipretide was 5000-fold enriched in the mitochondria when compared to the buffer.
L1123: Effect of Elamipretide on Mitochondrial Structure and Function in BTHS Related Models
L1124: Elamipretide was evaluated in various in vitro and in vivo models relevant to BTHS (i.e., models with TAZ deficiency and elevated MLCL/CL ratios). The in vitro models included lymphoblastoid cells (LCLs) isolated from subjects with BTHS; induced pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes (iPSC-CM) from subjects with BTHS; and human embryonic kidney cells with knockdown of the expression of the TAZ gene (HEK293-TAZKD). The in vivo study
L1125: used a transgenic mouse model with a knockdown of the TAZ gene, in which the mitochondrial MLCL/CL ratio is elevated.
L1126: LCL Cells (Study SPI-JHU-20-01)
L1127: In LCL cells from BTHS subjects, complex I (i.e., NADH:Ubiquinone oxidoreductase) activity, expression of NADH:Ubiquinone oxidoreductase subunit B6 (NDUFB6), cytochrome c oxidase IV (COXIV), a complex IV subunit, and SDHA, a complex II subunit, were decreased. Elamipretide (100nM) did not restore the expression of NDUFB6, COXIV and SDHA, and the effect of elamipretide on complex I activity was not measured.
L1128: HEK293-TAZKD Cells (Study SPI-JHU-20-01)
L1129: In HEK293-TAZKD cells, complex I activity, protein expression of NDUFB6, and relative abundance of NDUFB6 to COXIV and NDUFB6 to SDHA were decreased when compared to the cells without TAZ-knockdown. Incubation with elamipretide at a concentration of 100nM did not significantly alter the relative protein expression of NDUFB6. The effect of elamipretide on complex I activity was not evaluated in this model.
L1130: The study reported that TAZ-knockdown in HEK293 cells decreased gene expression of complex I subunits including NDUFB2, NDUFB4 and NDUFB6 and NDUFAB1. Elamipretide significantly increased gene expression of NDUFB4 and NDUFAB1. However, in the absence of changes in protein expression, the significance of increased gene expression is unknown.
L1131: iPSC-CM (Study SPI-BCH-16-01)
L1132: Effect of elamipretide (1μM) on oxygen consumption rate was evaluated in iPSC-derived cardiomyocytes. The Applicant stated that elamipretide qualitatively improved oxygen consumption rate under maximum respiration condition based on a graph (Table 62), which was presented at a conference in 2016 (Wang 2016b). However, no primary data were provided in the Applicant’s study report and no peer-reviewed publication describing the results could be located.

L1137: TAZ-Knockdown Mice (Russo et al. 2022), (Russo et al. 2024)
L1138: In the TAZ-knockdown mouse model reported by Russo et al. 2022, increased MLCL/CL ratio, decreased mitochondrial state 3 and state 4 respiration and maximum respiratory rate, as well as increased number of mitochondria with large vacuoles and loss in cristae canonical structure and reduced cristae density (under transmission electron microscopy), were observed prior to treatment. Cardiac dysfunction was not established in the model. Subcutaneous administration of once daily (3 mg/kg/day) elamipretide (labeled as SS-31 in Table 63) for 10 weeks improved cardiac mitochondrial state 3 and state 4 respiration (Table 63) and showed a trend for improvement for maximum respiratory rate.

L1146: In another study using the same TAZ-knockdown mouse model, once daily subcutaneous administration of elamipretide (3 mg/kg/day) for 10 weeks decreased the number of vacuolated mitochondria and improved cristae density suggesting an improvement in mitochondria morphology (Table 64).

L1156: Effect of Elamipretide on Cardiac Structure and Function in BTHS Related Models
L1157: In a different TAZ-knockdown mouse study (Study SPI-BCH-16-01) described by the Applicant, TAZ-knockdown in mice was induced immediately following birth. Knockdown reduced TAZ mRNA by &gt;89% in cardiac and skeletal muscle (data not provided). Echocardiograms indicated that mice developed cardiomyopathy (data not shown) by 8 weeks of age. Elamipretide (5 mg/kg/day via Alzet pump) was administered starting at week 8 for 6 weeks. The Applicant provided figures from results they claim showed that elamipretide improved fractional shortening over 6 weeks and decreased extent of fibrosis and increased ventricular diameter (Table 65). The Applicant's figures were presented at a conference in 2016 (Wang 2016a), but no data were provided in the Applicant's submitted report and no peer-reviewed publication describing these results was located. The effect on fractional shortening appeared insignificant based on the graph and the significance of treatment effect on ventricular fibrosis could not be determined based on one image. The Applicant also acknowledged that "These changes were not significant across the full cohort." Although the Applicant attributed lack of significant effects to variability in sex and genotype (homozygotes versus heterozygotes) in the TAZ-knockdown model, no explanation for the study design or evidence to support the hypothesis were provided.

L1162: Effect of Elamipretide on MLCL/CL Ratio in BTHS Related Models
L1163: LCL Cells (Study SPI-JHU-20-01)
L1164: The MLCL/CL ratio in TAZ null and hypomorph LCL cells ranged from 5.5-7.9 and 4.0-5.5, respectively. The MLCL/CL ratio in the elamipretide treated cells (100nM; 9-15 days) varied from 4.6-7.6 and 4.8 to 5.5, respectively. Incubation with elamipretide had no significant effect on MLCL/CL ratio.
L1165: TAZ-Knockdown Mouse Models
L1166: • TAZ-knockdown reduced CL by 90% and increased MLCL approximately 10-fold. Elamipretide (5 mg/kg/day; 6 weeks) did not alter the MLCL:CL ratio (Study SPI-BCH-16-01)
• In the TAZ-knockdown mouse model reported by Russo et al. 2022, MLCL/CL ratio was robustly elevated when compared to wild type control. Elamipretide (3 mg/kg/day; 10 weeks) did not alter the MLCL/CL ratio (Table 66).

L1172: Effect of Elamipretide in Fibroblasts Isolated From Patients With Dilated Cardiomyopathy With Ataxia Syndrome (Machiraju et al. 2019)
L1173: Dilated cardiomyopathy with ataxia syndrome (DCMA) is a rare and autosomal recessive genetic disorder characterized by mitochondria that are highly fragmented. DCMA is considered related to BTHS with respect to cardiac pathology and urinary secretion of 3-methylglutaconic acid. However, the disorder is due to mutations in the DNAJC19 gene (a gene that encodes mitochondrial import inner membrane translocase subunit [TIM4]), and not to mutations in the TAZ gene.
L1174: Dermal fibroblasts were isolated from patients with DCMA. In the DCMA fibroblasts, mitochondrial networks were fragmented and disorganized with thinner cristae when compared to intact and reticular mitochondrial networks in control cells. Mitochondrial reactive oxygen species production was also higher in DCMA fibroblasts. Elamipretide (100nM; 24 hrs) qualitatively improved mitochondrial networks as indicated by reduced fragmentation and increased branching of mitochondrial networks and decreased mitochondrial reactive oxygen species (ROS) production.
L1175: Effect of Elamipretide in Non-BTHS Models
L1176: Elamipretide was tested in other animal models that were not specifically relevant to BTHS, which included models of heart failure, ischemia-reperfusion (I/R) injury, skeletal muscle atrophy, hemorrhagic shock, and LPS-induced injury. The relevance of these studies to BTHS is unclear, given that the TAZ gene remains intact and MLCL/CL ratio was relatively unaffected in these models. The effects of elamipretide on mitochondrial function and structure, as well as cardiac functional endpoints, in these models are discussed below.
L1177: Effect on Mitochondrial Structure/Function and Cardiac Function
L1178: Dogs
L1179: • Chronic heart failure in dogs was induced by serial intracoronary microembolization until left ventricle ejection fraction (as determined by angiography) was 30% (Sabbah et al. 2016). At 6 weeks after last microembolization, animals showed:– Increased left ventricle end-diastolic pressure, systemic vascular resistance, left ventricular end-diastolic circumferential wall stress, plasma levels of n-terminal pro brain natriuretic peptide, tumor necrosis factor-\alpha, interleukin-6, C-reactive protein, plasma and left ventricular ROS and 4-hydroxy 2-nonenal (4-HNE) levels in the left ventricle.– Decreased left ventricular ejection fraction, stroke volume, cardiac output, and cardiac index, complex I and IV activity, protein expression of endothelial nitric oxide synthase (eNOS), peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor coactivator-1\alpha, sarcoplasmic reticulum Ca2+-ATPase-2a, total cardiolipin and total (18:2)4CL subspecies.– Decreased mitochondrial respiration, mitochondrial membrane potential, ATP synthesis, ratio of ATP/ADP in the cardiomyocytes of the left ventricle and expression of complex I, II, III, IV and V subunits in the left ventricular tissue.• At 6 weeks after last microembolization when heart failure was established, treatment with elamipretide (0.5 mg/kg/day, SC for 3 months resulted in the following improvements compared to the dogs with heart failure that received the vehicle control:– Improved cardiac function and related biomarkers (left ventricle end-diastolic pressure, LVEF, fractional shortening, systemic vascular resistance, left ventricular end-diastolic circumferential wall stress, n-terminal pro brain natriuretic peptide, tumor necrosis factor-\alpha, interleukin-6, C-reactive protein, ROS and HNE).– Normalized the total CL and (18:2)4CL levels.– Increased protein expression of complex I, II, and IV subunits (data not shown).– Increased Complex I and Complex IV activities.– Improved mitochondrial functioning as indicated by mitochondrial respiration, mitochondrial membrane potential, ATP synthesis, and ratio of ATP/ADP ratio (Table 67) (Sabbah et al. 2016).

L1188: Rats
L1189: Heart failure in rats was induced by myocardial infarction caused by ligation of the left coronary artery (Dai et al. 2014). Occlusion was confirmed by cyanosis and akinesis of the left ventricular anterior wall. Subcutaneous administration of elamipretide (3 mg/kg/day) was started 2 hours post occlusion and continued for 6 weeks.
Elamipretide (also named as Bendavia) treatment:

Improved the endpoints for cardiac function when compared to the vehicle control, including left ventricular fractional shortening (23.8% versus 28.8%), left ventricular stroke volume (0.23 mL versus 0.26 mL) and left ventricular ejection fraction (49.3% versus 55.3%).
Decreased the incidence of apoptotic cardiomyocytes and reactive oxygen species production.
Increased complex I (data not shown) and complex IV activities (Table 68).
Caused no treatment related changes in the non-infarcted remote left ventricular region.

L1196: • Cardiac injury in the isolated rat heart was induced global ischemia for 20 minutes followed by reperfusion for 2 hours (Allen et al. 2020). Mitochondrial function was evaluated in ventricular muscle fibers. Following I/R injury, mitochondrial respiration across complexes I-IV was decreased. Elamipretide (10μM) infused from the beginning of reperfusion and continuing for 120 minutes ameliorated I/R-induced decreases in mitochondrial oxygen consumption as indicated by improved Complex I (state 3) and Complex II (state 3) activities. Treatment with elamipretide also resulted in enhanced mitochondrial cristae connections (Table 69). Total cardiolipin and the most abundant cardiolipin species in heart (18:2-18:2-18:2-18:2) were decreased following I/R injury. Elamipretide did not abrogate these decreases.

L1208: Isolated Guinea Pig Hearts
L1209: Effects of elamipretide (labeled as SS-31) on contractile function were evaluated in isolated guinea pig hearts following I/R injury (Study PD-001). Endpoints measured included contractile force, heart rate, and total coronary flow. Mitochondrial function was not studied. A significant reduction in contractile force was noted following I/R in the buffer-only (vehicle) group when compared to pre-ischemia. Preischemic or postischemic treatment with elamipretide (1nM or 100nM) significantly improved contractile force. No dose-dependency was noted (Table 70). Elamipretide did not affect heart rate and coronary flow.

L1218: Mice
L1219: Pressure load heart failure in mice was induced by transverse aortic constriction for 4 weeks (Dai et al. 2013). Echocardiography after 4 weeks of transverse aortic constriction demonstrated heart failure characterized by increased left ventricular mass index, increased left ventricular end-diastolic dimension (LVED), and decreased fractional shortening. Heart failure increased heart weight (normalized to tibia), fibrosis, collagen 1a2, mitochondrial protein carbonyl content and light chain 3-II (LC3-II). Elamipreptide treatment for 4 weeks (3 mg/kg/day):
      
Did not alter left ventricular mass index, but significantly improved fractional shortening and attenuated LVED diameter.
Reduced heart weight, fibrosis, and collagen 1a2 (Table 71).
Decreased heart failure-induced increases in mitochondrial protein carbonyl content and LC3-II protein expression.
Attenuated the heart failure-related changes in expression of proteins involved in actin cytoskeleton, mitochondrial function, intermediate metabolism, glycolysis/gluconeogenesis, and citrate cycle.

L1228: Effect of Elamipretide on Apoptosis/Necrosis in Models Not Related to BTHS
L1229: • In the rat heart failure model induced by myocardial infarction, elamipretide (3 mg/kg/day; 6 weeks) also decreased apoptotic markers (e.g., terminal deoxynucleotide transferase-mediated dUTP nick-end labeling) in the area proximal to the myocardial infarction.
• Effect of elamipretide (labeled as MTP-131; up to 1nM) on infarct size was evaluated in the isolated guinea pig hearts (Studies PD-002 and SPIRIIV-N109) and anesthetized rabbits (in vivo) [Study #s SPIRIIV-N103, SPIRIIV-N112, SPI-QTE-10-01, and SPIRIIV-N110] following myocardial I/R injury. Elamipretide (1nM) improved coronary flow and decreased apoptosis in the ventricles as indicated by TUNEL staining) (Table 72). Effects of elamipretide on mitochondrial function were not evaluated in these studies.

L1236: Effect of elamipretide on LPS-induced mitochondrial dysfunction was studied in mice. LPS (2 \mu\text{g}) was stereotaxically infused into the lateral ventricle of mice once daily for three consecutive days (Zhao et al. 2019). LPS increased ROS production and apoptosis and decreased mitochondrial membrane potential, and ATP levels in the hippocampus. Elamipretide (labeled as SS-31; 5 mg/kg) administered intraperitoneally 30 minutes before administration of LPS improved these parameters in the mice (Table 73).

L1245: • The effect of elamipretide metabolites (labeled SPI-09-No.29 and SPI-09-No.30) on isolated guinea pig heart was studied following I/R injury (Study SPI-ECU-10-01). Administration of SPI-09-No.29 (0.1nM and 1μM) and SPI-09-No.30 (0.1nM and 1μM) prior to ischemia or at the beginning of reperfusion did not affect the infarct size, suggesting that metabolites of elamipretide were not pharmacologically active in the I/R model. The effect of elamipretide on mitochondrial function was not evaluated in this study.
L1246: Effect of Elamipretide on Mitochondrial Function in Skeletal Muscles
L1247: • The effect of elamipretide (3 mg/kg) on mitochondria was studied in young (5-months of age) and old (27-months of age) mice following a single intraperitoneal dose (Siegel et al. 2013). Skeletal muscle (gastrocnemius) mitochondrial bioenergetics were evaluated within an hour following administration of elamipretide. Glutathione was lower in gastrocnemius muscle of aged mice controls when compared to young mice. H2O2 production was higher in the muscle of aged mice controls when compared to young mice. Injection of elamipretide to aged mice improved glutathione, reduced H2O2 production, and HNE (a marker of apoptosis). Administration of elamipretide daily for 8 days increased whole body endurance in aged mice.• Skeletal muscle atrophy was induced by muscle immobilization in mice for 14 days. Elamipretide (1.5 mg/kg) was administered daily subcutaneously for 14 days (Min et al. 2011). Mitochondrial ROS production was evaluated in permeabilized muscle fibers isolated from soleus and plantaris muscles. Muscle immobilization increased H2O2 production, 4-HNE adducts, activity of calpain-1 and caspase-3, and muscle atrophy. Muscle immobilization reduced mitochondrial function as indicated by the altered respiratory control ratio. Elamipretide decreased H2O2 production, 4-HNE adducts, reduced activity of calpain-1 and caspase-3 and prevented mitochondrial uncoupling and muscle atrophy.• Skeletal muscle was isolated from dogs subjected to chronic intracoronary microembolization-induced heart failure (Sabbah et al. 2019). Elamipretide (0.5 mg/kg) was administered subcutaneously for 3 months. The proportion of type 1 (oxidative, slow contracting) and type 2 fibers (glycolytic, fast contracting) and cross-sectional area was assessed in triceps muscle. Mitochondrial functions [ADP-dependent mitochondrial respiration, maximum rate of ATP synthesis, cytochrome C oxidase activity, mitochondrial membrane potential (\Delta\psi_m), and mitochondrial permeability transition pore] were evaluated in the isolated mitochondria. Heart failure reduced type 1 skeletal fibers whereas type 2 fibers were slightly increased. Elamipretide restored the ratio of these fibers but did not affect the fiber cross-sectional area. Heart failure reduced ADP-stimulated mitochondrial respiration, \Delta\psi_m, mitochondrial permeability transition pore opening, maximum rate of ATP synthesis, ATP/ADP ratio, and cytochrome C oxidase activity in the skeletal muscles. Elamipretide (0.1 to 1μM) improved all these parameters in skeletal muscles isolated from heart failure dogs.
L1248: Effect of Elamipretide on MLCL/CL Ratio in Non-BTHS Models
L1249: The effect of elamipretide on the MLCL/CL ratio was evaluated in young (5-month-old) and aged (26-month-old) mice. Based on the graph provided in the publication, the estimated MLCL/CL ratio in young, untreated mice was 0.003 and increased to 0.01 in aged, untreated mice (Table 74). The estimated MLCL/CL ratio in aged mice treated with elamipretide was 0.006. Treatment with elamipretide (3 mg/kg/day) for 8 weeks did not significantly improve the
L1250: MLCL/CL ratio in old mice 213WQ. Further, the MLCL/CL ratio in untreated, young mice and aged mice are 0.003 and 0.01, respectively, which appears in the range of normal mice (&lt;0.01, [Russo et al. 2022], and may not be relevant to BTHS, where the MLCL/CL ratio is robustly elevated (e.g., &gt;5 in TAZ-knockdown mice). Therefore, any effect of elamipretide on the MLCL/CL ratio in aged mice is not considered translatable to BTHS. It is possible that elamipretide stabilizes CL and increases its half-life, but no empirical data are available to confirm this hypothesis.

L1258: 13.1.2. Secondary Pharmacology
L1259: A secondary pharmacology screen with 87 receptors (Cerep Panlab) did not reveal significant activity (as indicated by more than 50% inhibition) for elamipretide (10\muM) or its M1 (tripeptide) and M2 (bipeptide) metabolites (10\muM). Inhibition of neuropeptide YY1 (40-66%) and human opiate \kappa (46-67%) was variable between experiments at 10\muM elamipretide. The clinical C_{max} (1.37 \mug/mL) is 5-times lower than the inhibitory concentration (6.4 \mug/mL; 10\muM) of elamipretide, therefore, clinical effects at these receptors are considered unlikely.
L1260: No significant activity of elamipretide was noted in a panel containing 388 kinases. Vasopressin-like effects with dose-limiting hyponatremia (high urinary sodium in the presence of low serum sodium) was noted preclinically and in the first-in-human clinical study, suggesting possible syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion. Therefore, we recommended the Applicant evaluate the activity of elamipretide and metabolites on vasopressin receptors. No significant activity of elamipretide and M1 and M2 was noted against vasopressin receptors. Additionally, the initial concern for hyponatremia was not borne out in subsequent clinical trials.

L1267: 13.1.4. Pharmacokinetics and Toxicokinetics
L1268: 13.1.4.1. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion
L1269: The bioavailability of elamipretide following subcutaneous administration in rats (30-50%) was lower than in dogs, monkeys, and pigs (&gt;90%). In vitro plasma protein-binding assays indicated that the protein binding of elamipretide in dogs and humans was approximately 45%. Intravenous studies indicated that elamipretide is predominantly present in plasma, but exposures in kidney were 50-fold higher than plasma. Minimal amounts of elamipretide were noted in the eyes and brain.
L1270: Elamipretide undergoes C-terminal degradation to a tripeptide, M1 (D-Arg-Dmt-Lys-OH), and then to a dipeptide, M2 (D-Arg-Dmt-OH; Table 76). Exposure to M1 and M2 relative to elamipretide exceeded 10% of total exposure in humans, rats, and dogs. M1 and M2 were determined as major metabolites of elamipretide based on human data (see Section 14 Clinical Pharmacology). The AUCs for elamipretide, M1, and M2 at the MRHD in humans (Study SPISC-102) were 3850, 3110, and 1250 ng*hr/mL, respectively.
L1271: No significant metabolites were detected when elamipretide was incubated with mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human hepatic microsomes. Therefore, cytochrome P450 enzymes are unlikely to be involved in elamipretide's metabolism. Elamipretide (up to 200µM) did not inhibit cytochrome P450 enzymes including 1A2, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1. Weak inhibition was noted for 3A34 at concentrations greater than 200µM.
L1272: M1 and M2 were tested at doses up to 1\muM in an I/R model (Study SPI-ECU-10-01) and did not show effect in reduction of infarct size. A positive control or elamipretide was not tested in the same study. No data are available regarding the effects of M1 and M2 on mitochondrial function.
L1273: Exposures to M1 and M2 based on a 26-week repeat-dose study in rats were 14- and 2-fold higher than human M1 and M2 exposure (AUC) at the MRHD. Similarly, exposures to M1 and M2 (at systemic NOAEL) based on a 39-week repeat-dose study in dogs was 1.8- and 4-fold higher than human M1 and M2 exposure (AUC) at the MRHD. Metabolite M1 and M2 are negative for mutagenicity based on the results of Ames assay (Studies SPI-COV-11-01_SPI-09-NO.29 and SPI-COV-11-01_SPI-09-No.30). Overall, the abundance of metabolites M1 and M2 in animals covered human exposures at the MRHD (see review of pivotal toxicology studies in the sections below).
L1274: Table 76. Proposed Metabolic Pathway Depicted by the Applicant
L1275: The diagram illustrates the metabolic pathway of elamipretide (MTP-131). The starting molecule, MTP-131, is a tripeptide with a mitochondrial targeting sequence (MTP) and a C-terminal arginine. It has a molecular weight of m/z 640.392. The diagram shows two degradation pathways: one leading to metabolite M1 (m/z 494.309) and another leading to metabolite M2 (m/z 366.214). M1 is the C-terminal degradation product of elamipretide, and M2 is the dipeptide C-terminal degradation product of elamipretide. The structures show the loss of the MTP sequence and the formation of the degradation products.Chemical reaction diagram showing the metabolic pathway of elamipretide (MTP-131) to metabolites M1 and M2. The diagram shows the chemical structures of MTP-131, M1, and M2. MTP-131 is a tripeptide with a mitochondrial targeting sequence (MTP) and a C-terminal arginine. M1 is the C-terminal degradation product of elamipretide, and M2 is the dipeptide C-terminal degradation product of elamipretide. The diagram shows the conversion of MTP-131 to M1 and M2.
L1276: Source: Excerpted from Applicant's Pharmacokinetics summary
L1277: Abbreviations: M1, tripeptide C-terminus degradation product of elamipretide; M2, dipeptide C-terminus degradation product of elamipretide; MTP-131, elamipretide
L1278: Studies in rats using radiolabeled elamipretide, M1 and M2, indicated that all were primarily excreted in the urine. Minimal excretion was noted in the feces.
L1279: 13.1.4.2. Toxicokinetic Data
L1280: Pharmacokinetics of elamipretide were evaluated following single and repeat-dose administration in rats, dogs, monkeys, and pigs. Elamipretide was rapidly absorbed and eliminated following subcutaneous administration (terminal half-life 0.25 to 1.3 hrs). Exposure to elamipretide increased in a dose-dependent manner in rats and dogs (up to 10 mg/kg). Shown in Table 77 are the pharmacokinetics of elamipretide following single intravenous and subcutaneous routes of administration. Shown in Table 78 are the toxicokinetics of elamipretide in the repeat-dose toxicity studies.

L1304: 13.1.5.1.1. General Toxicity Study in Rats
L1305: A 26-Week Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With a 4-Week Recovery in Rats (Study SPI-CIT-15-03)
L1306: Key Study Findings
L1307: Injection site reactions, including redness and scab formation, were noted in all test-article groups, with increased incidence and severity at the high dose. Elamipretide doses were reduced in all dose groups at Day 8, i.e., 10 mg/kg/day to 5 mg/kg/day; 20 mg/kg/day to 10 mg/kg/day; and 40 mg/kg/day to 15 mg/kg/day. Injection site reactions were less prevalent after dose reduction but persisted in all groups.
Clinical signs were related to skin reactions in both control and treated animals. Gross lesions were confined to the sites of injection in all groups. Injections site reactions were exacerbated in treated groups. Thus, the lesions are considered both procedure and test-article related.
L1308: Histopathology of skin lesions included minimal to moderate subcutaneous hemorrhage, inflammation, fibrosis, and myofiber degeneration/regeneration and/or minimal to mild epidermis crust formation. Severity of injection site reactions were lower in the recovery group. Except for fibrosis, other histopathology lesions were not present in the recovery group.
L1309: • No other significant test article-related adverse effect on the measured parameters were observed.
• The NOAEL for local toxicity is the low dose of 10/5 mg/kg/day. The sex combined C_{max} AUC_{last} at this NOAEL are 3650 ng/mL and 7795 ng*hr/mL, respectively, approximately 2-fold AUC_{last} at the MRHD.
• The NOAEL for systemic toxicity is the high dose of 40/15 mg/kg/day. The sex combined C_{max} and AUC_{last} were 6875 ng/mL and 23850 ng*hr/mL, respectively, approximately 6-fold AUC_{last} at the MRHD.
• At the NOAEL of 40/15 mg/kg/day for systemic toxicity, the AUC_{last} for the major human metabolites, M1 and M2, were 43000 ng*hr/mL and 2470 ng*hr/mL, respectively, approximately 14-fold and 2-fold the AUC_{last} of M1 and M2, respectively, at the MRHD.

L1314: Table 81. Observations and Results of 26-Week Subcutaneous Toxicity Study in Rats
L1315: Endpoints with Notable ObservationsMajor Findings



MortalitySingle incidence of death was noted in control (female, day 106), low dose (male, day 143) and high dose (male, day 200) groups. Cause of death in the control animal was lymphoma in the bone. For the low and high dose animals, clinical signs were limited to skin lesions; the exact cause of death was not determined. Since no increasing trend of mortality was noted in the treated groups when compared to the control, the mortality observed is unlikely to be test article related.

Clinical signsSkin lesions at injection site (redness and scab formation) were noted in all test groups with increased frequency and severity in high dose groups. These changes were less prevalent after the dose was reduced, however, present in all groups including controls.
L1316: Endpoints with Notable ObservationsMajor Findings



HistopathologyAdequate battery:YesSkin lesions at injection site were observed in control and all treated groups, which included minimal to moderate subcutaneous hemorrhage, inflammation, fibrosis, and myofiber degeneration/regeneration and/or minimal to mild epidermis crust formation. Incidence and severity of lesions in treated groups were higher than that in the control group and appeared dose dependent. Severity of injection site reactions were lower in the recovery group. Incidence and severity of fibrosis was partially reversed in recovery group. All other lesions were fully resolved at the end of recovery period. The injection site skin lesions were considered both procedure- and test-article related.

Endpoints without notable observationsNo dose-dependent changes in body weights, food consumption, ophthalmology parameters, hematology parameters, coagulation parameters, clinical chemistry, urinalysis parameters. Incidental organ weights were not accompanied with histopathological correlates.
L1317: Source: Excerpted from Study SPI-CIT-15-03
L1318: Toxicokinetics
L1319: Elamipretide is rapidly absorbed (time to maximum concentration [T_{max}]: 0.5 h) and with a half-life of 0.6-1.5 h. A dose-proportional increase in exposures to elamipretide and metabolites (M1 and M2) were noted. The AUC_{last} of M1 and M2 was approximately 14-fold and 2-fold higher, respectively, than clinical exposures at the MRHD. The toxicokinetic parameters are shown in Table 82.

L1324: General Toxicity Study With Shorter Durations in Rats
L1325: Toxicity of elamipretide has also been evaluated in 28-day and 90-day daily repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and are summarized below.
L1326: 28-Day Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Rats (Study MTP-131-TOX008; GLP: Yes)
L1327: • Elamipretide (0, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day) was daily administered subcutaneously for 28 days. A 14-day recovery group was included for control and high dose.• Injection site reactions were noted in all groups including controls. Skin discoloration was noted in all treatment groups. Severity and incidence of skin lesions increased with dose and dosing days. Histopathologic evaluation of skin lesions at the injection site included skin edema, erythema, scab, sloughing, discoloration and myofiber degeneration. Most of the skin lesions were resolved in the recovery group.• Decreased thymus weights (absolute and relative to brain) accompanied with minimal to mild lymphoid depletion were noted in high dose males (7/10) and females (2/10). No microscopic lesions were noted in the lower dose groups. No test article related histopathological changes were noted in the recovery group animals.• The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was 30 mg/kg/day due to thymus weight decrease accompanied with lymphoid depletion at 100 mg/kg/day, likely related to stress. A NOAEL for local toxicity could not be established.
L1328: 90-Day Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study Followed by a 28-Day Recovery Period in Rats (Study SPI-RIC-13-03; GLP: Yes)
L1329: • Elamipretide (0, 0.3, 1, 3, and 6 mg/rat) was administered subcutaneously daily for 90 days.• Injection site reactions (red and/or dark discoloration of the subcutaneous tissue) were noted at \geq 1 mg/day. Histopathology of skin lesions at the injection site included perivascular mononuclear or mixed cell infiltrates (\geq 1 mg/day), hemorrhage (\geq 0.3 mg/day), muscle necrosis (\geq 3 mg/day), muscle degeneration (\geq 3 mg/day), and collagen lysis (\geq 3 mg/day). Complete or near complete recovery of injection site reactions except perivascular mononuclear or mixed cell infiltrates were noted in all groups by postdose Day 6.• The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was 6 mg/rat (10-15 mg/kg/day in males and 20-25 mg/kg/day in females), based on the absence of significant toxicity at the highest dose. At the NOAEL, the C_{max} values for males and females are 6325 ng/mL and 10,080 ng/mL, respectively; and the AUC_{last} values for males and females are 15,400 and 24,800 ng*hr/mL, respectively. A NOAEL for local toxicity could not be determined, based on adverse injection site reactions at all doses.
L1330: 13.1.5.1.2. General Toxicity in Dogs
L1331: A 39-Week Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With a 4-Week Recovery in Dogs (Study SPI-CIT-15-02)
L1332: Key Study Findings
L1333: • Clinical signs of discomfort, including redness of the ear canal, pinna, abdomen, penis, muzzle, and periorbital regions, and swelling of the lip, periorbital regions, pinna, muzzle, and ear canal, and excessive licking (starting on Day 2) and vocalization (starting from day 20 and 26) were observed. As these signs did not subside with continued dosing, the mid and high doses were reduced to 5 (from 10 mg/kg/day) and 10 mg/kg/day (from 20 mg/kg/day) from day 79/80 through the remainder of the treatment period. No dose adjustment was made for the low dose group. Antihistamines were administered to relieve discomfort in individual animals. The clinical signs were alleviated following dosing modifications, however, some of them were present until the end of study. Administration of antihistamine did not alter the toxicokinetic profile of elamipretide.
• Gross changes were noted at the sites of injection and were accompanied by histopathological changes including inflammation in the mid and high dose (minimal to marked in severity) and control (minimal to moderate) groups. Increased incidence and severity of hemorrhage (minimal to severe) was noted in the high dose group when compared to lower doses and controls (minimal to moderate in severity). Hemorrhage was completely resolved at the end of the 4-week recovery period whereas inflammation was partially resolved.
• The NOAEL for local toxicity was 2.5 mg/kg/day, whereas the NOAEL for systemic toxicity was 20/10 mg/kg/day. The exposures (AUC_{\text{last}}) to elamipretide, M1 and M2 at the NOAEL for systemic toxicity was approximately 5.7-fold, 1.8-fold and 4.1-fold the AUC at the MRHD, respectively.
L1334: Table 83. Methods of 39-Week Subcutaneous Study in Dogs
L1335: Study Features and MethodsDescription



GLP complianceYes

Doses and frequency of dosing0, 2.5, 10, and 20 mg/kg/day, once daily. Due to skin lesions, from day 79/80, the dose levels had been reduced for mid dose group from 10 mg/kg/day to 5 mg/kg/day and for high dose group from 20 mg/kg/day to 10 mg/kg/day. Animals in the mid and high-dose group were given a dosing holiday sporadically for a total of 29 days, which constituted about 10% of total study duration. Additional details on the exact dosing holidays were not provided. Toxicokinetics (C_{\text{max}} and AUC_{\text{last}}) were evaluated on days 1, 133, and 273. No significant differences were noted at day 133 and 273. The sporadic dosing holiday in a 39-week dosing period was not considered to have significant impact on the data interpretation or conclusion of the study.

L1339: Table 84. Observations and Results of 39-Week Subcutaneous Toxicity Study in Dogs
L1340: Endpoints With Notable ObservationsMajor Findings



MortalityNone

Clinical signsIn the mid and high dose groups, redness of the ear canal, pinna, abdomen, penis, muzzle, and periorbital regions was observed within few minutes of dosing, swelling of the lip, periorbital regions, pinna, muzzle, and ear canal, and excessive licking was noted from Day 2, and vocalizations were noted from Day 20 and Day 26, respectively. These signs were transient and fully reversible after administration of test article but represented discomfort for the animals. Antihistamines were administered to reduce histamine-like responses. Nonetheless, signs of discomfort were noted occasionally. Reduction in doses reduced the incidence and/or severity of injection site reaction.

ImmunologyIgG, IgE and histamine levels were monitored in control and high dose groups. Increased histamine levels were noted at 5 to 15 minutes post dose in the high dose group when compared to control. Histamine levels were comparable to control within 30 minutes post dose. No changes in circulating levels of IgG and IgE were noted in the high dose group. IgG and IgE levels were lower in both control and high dose groups at the end of the dosing period when compared to predose.

HistopathologyHistopathological changes were noted at the injection site in control and treated groups. Increased severity was noted in the treated groups when compared to control.

Adequate battery: YesMild to moderate inflammation was noted in a majority of treated animals whereas a majority of control animals had minimal inflammation. Increased incidence and severity of hemorrhages were noted in the treated group when compared to control (Table 85). A clear dose-response was not observed for inflammation and hemorrhage. In recovery groups, hemorrhage was fully resolved whereas minimal to mild inflammation was still present in both treated and control animals (data not shown).

L1358: General Toxicity Study With Shorter Durations in Dogs
L1359: Toxicity of elamipretide had also been evaluated in 28-day and 90-day daily repeat-dose toxicity studies in dogs and are summarized below.
L1360: 28-Day Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Beagle Dogs (Study MTP-131-TOX-002; GLP: Yes)
L1361: • Elamipretide (0, 1, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day) was administered once-daily subcutaneously for 28 days. A 14-day recovery group was included for all doses.• Intermittent/continuous tremors and decreased activity were noted in the 10 and 30 mg/kg/day groups in a dose-dependent manner starting 15 to 30 minutes after dosing and persisting for 1 to 2 hours. Reddened and swollen body surface was also noted in the 10 and 30 mg/kg groups, which resolved prior to dosing on the following day. No significant clinical signs were noted during the recovery period.• Injection site reactions were noted in all dose groups. Histopathology of injection sites included inflammation, necrosis, hemorrhages, and myofiber damage, which were partially resolved at the end of the recovery period.• No test article-related deaths, changes in body weights, feed consumption, hematology, urinalysis, electrocardiogram (ECG), and organ weights were noted.• The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was 1 mg/kg/day. The C_{max} and AUC_{inf} at the systemic NOAEL were 1594 ng/mL and 1829 ng*hr/mL, respectively. A NOAEL for local toxicity could not be determined.
L1362: 90-Day Repeat-Dose Subcutaneous Toxicity Study in Dogs (Study SPI-RIC-13-04; GLP: Yes)
L1363: • Elamipretide (0, 1, 3, 10, and 20 mg/day) was administered once-daily subcutaneously for 90 days.• Injection site reactions (discoloration of the subcutaneous tissue) were noted at all doses levels, with higher incidence in males in the 10 mg/kg/day group. Histopathologic findings associated with injection site reactions included perivascular mononuclear cell infiltrates inflammation, muscle degeneration, fibrosis/fibroplasia, hemorrhage, and seroma. Except for perivascular mononuclear cell infiltration and muscle degeneration, all lesions resolved by postdose Day 10.• The systemic NOAEL was 20 mg/day (2.4 mg/kg/day). The C_{max} and AUC_{last} at the systemic NOAEL are 2828 ng/mL and 4520 ng*hr/mL, respectively. A NOAEL for local toxicity could not be established.
L1364: 13.1.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L1365: Table 87. Genotoxicity Studies
L1366: StudyFindings



Study MTP-131-TOX007Title: Bacterial reverse mutation assayMethod: Mutagenicity potential of elamipretide was tested in S. typhimurium (strain TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537) and E. coli (strain WP2 uvrA) using plate incorporation method in the presence and absence of metabolic activation.Elamipretide was not mutagenic in a standard and valid GLP-compliant Ames assay at concentrations up to 1500 µg/plate.

Study MTP-131-TOX007Title: In vitro chromosomal aberration testMethod: Clastogenicity potential of elamipretide was evaluated in vitro in CHO cells in the presence (4 hours) and absence of metabolic activation (4-20 hrs).Elamipretide was negative in a standard and valid GLP-compliant chromosomal aberration assay at concentrations up to 5000 µg/mL.

Study MTP-131-TOX007Title: MTP-131: Rat bone marrow erythrocyte micronucleus testMethod: Genotoxicity potential of elamipretide was evaluated in vivo in micronucleated polychromatic erythrocytes in the bone marrow of rats following a single dose (125, 500 and 500 mg/kg) via subcutaneous route.Elamipretide tested negative in a standard and valid GLP-compliant in vivo micronucleus assay in rats following single subcutaneous doses (up to 500 mg/kg).

Study SPI-COV-11-01Title: MTP-131: SPI-09-No.29: Bacterial Reverse Mutation assay: Treat and Plate Method with a Confirmatory AssayMethod: Mutagenicity potential of metabolite M1 was tested in S. typhimurium (strain TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537) and E. coli (strain WP2 uvrA) via treat and plate incorporation method in the presence and absence of metabolic activation.Metabolite M1 was not mutagenic in a standard and valid GLP-compliant Ames assay at concentrations up to 5000 µg/plate.

Study SPI-COV-11-02Title: MTP-131: SPI-09-No.30: Bacterial Reverse Mutation assay: Treat and Plate Method with a Confirmatory AssayMethod: Mutagenicity potential of metabolite M2 was tested in S. typhimurium (strain TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537) and E. coli (strain WP2 uvrA) via treat and plate incorporation method in the presence and absence of metabolic activation.Metabolite M2 was not mutagenic in a standard and valid GLP-compliant Ames assay at concentrations up to 5000 µg/plate.

L1382: 13.1.5.4.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
L1383: MTP-131: Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to Implantation Study in Rats(Study SPI-CRL-14-05; GLP: Yes)
L1384: Key Study Findings
L1385: • In males, no elamipretide-related adverse effects on mating and fertility, sperm motility or density and male organ weights were observed at doses tested up to 20 mg/kg/day.
• In females, no elamipretide-related adverse effects on the gestation period, estrus cycle, mating and fertility, mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, pre- or postimplantation loss or viability of embryos and female reproductive organ weights were observed at doses up to 20 mg/kg/day.
• The NOAEL for males and females was 20 mg/kg/day. The AUClast at the NOAEL resulted in an approximately 5-fold, 17-fold, and 2-fold clinical exposure to elamipretide, M1 and M2, respectively at the MRHD.
L1386: Table 89. Study Information, Study SPI-CRL-14-05
L1387: ParameterMethod Details



Dose and frequency of dosing0, 2, 6, 20 mg/kg/day, once daily

Route of administrationSubcutaneous

Formulation/vehicle0.9% Sodium chloride

Species/strainRats/Sprague-Dawley

Number/sex/group25/sex/group

Satellite groupsNo

Study design
In males, elamipretide was subcutaneously administered once-daily for 28 days prior to cohabitation, during cohabitation and continued until sacrifice. A total of 63 to 66 doses were administered.
In females, elamipretide was administered subcutaneously for 15 days prior to cohabitation, during cohabitation and continued until gestation day (GD) 7. Females were euthanized on GD 13.
Injection site reactions were noted in the 28 days, 90 days and 26 weeks repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats. In the 26-week repeat-dose toxicity study, administered doses of elamipretide were 10, 20 and 40 mg/kg/day. However, the doses were reduced by half from day 8 due to injection site reactions. The doses administered from day 8 until the end of the study were 5, 10 and 15 mg/kg/day. The highest dose in this study is reasonable.


Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of resultsNo significant deviations that impact the outcome of the study were noted.
L1388: Source: Excerpted from Study SPI-CRL-14-05
L1389: Table 90. Observations and Results From Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to Implantation in rats (Study SPI-CRL-14-05)
L1390: ParameterMajor Findings



MortalityEarly deaths were noted in the 2 mg/kg/day group (2 males) and 20 mg/kg/day group (one male). Deaths were due to accidental fall and gavage error (2 mg/kg/day) or oral injury (20 mg/kg/day). All tissues were normal at necropsy. Therefore, these deaths were not considered test article-related.

Clinical signsNo test article related clinical signs were noted in males and females.

Body weightsNo significant differences in body weight and body weight gains were noted in males and females.

Mating performance and fertility
In males, no differences for days needed for mating, mating and fertility were noted. In females, elamipretide did not affect days of cohabitation, mating (100%) and fertility index (76% in control versus 88% in all treatment groups).


Estrous cycleEstrous cycle stages were comparable among treatment groups.

Sperm analysisNo differences in vas deferens sperm motility and cauda epididymal sperm counts were noted in treated and control groups.

CaesareanNo test article-related differences in mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, pre- or postimplantation loss, or viable or nonviable embryos was noted. No placental abnormalities were also noted.

Organ weightsNo significant differences in absolute or relative male and female reproductive organ weights were noted in treated and control groups.

Necropsy findingsCaesarean Section dataNo test article-related changes were noted at necropsy.
L1391: ParameterMajor Findings



ToxicokineticsToxicokinetics were not evaluated in this study. The exposure (AUClast) at 20 mg/kg/day was estimated based on the 90-day repeat-dose toxicity study in rats. The Cmax in males and females was 6325 and 10080 ng/mL, respectively. The AUClast in males and females was 15400 and 24800 ng*hr/mL, respectively (Study SPI-RIC-13-03).
L1392: Source: Excerpted from Study SPI-CRL-14-05
L1393: Abbreviations: AUClast, area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to last measurable concentration, Cmax, maximum observed plasma concentration
L1394: 13.1.5.4.2. Embryofetal Development
L1395: Rat Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study
L1396: Preliminary Dose Range Finding Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Study in Rats (Study SPI-CRL-14-03; GLP: No)
L1397: A preliminary, dose range-finding, embryo-fetal development (EFD) toxicity study was conducted in timed-pregnant Sprague-Dawley rats (n=4) to support the dose selection in the definitive EFD study in rats. Elamipretide was intravenously infused (for an hour) from gestation day (GD) 7 to GD 17 at doses of 0, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day. The animals were sacrificed on day 21. Two high dose animals were found dead on the 1st and 2nd day of dosing (GD 7, GD 8). High dose group was terminated early due to severe toxicity. Marked decrease in body weight gain (67-82%) on GD 7 and GD 8 was noted in the low and mid dose groups. Body weight gain gradually recovered later. The body weight gain during GD 7-18 in the low and mid-dose groups was reduced 13-15% when compared to the control. Elamipretide postimplantation loss at 30 mg/kg/day was 9.8% (compared to 0% in control) but remained within historical control values. The high dose of 100 mg/kg/day was selected for the definitive EFD study in rats, which was considered reasonable based on maternal mortality and excessive toxicity at 30 mg/kg/day and &gt;10% body weight gain reduction.
L1398: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of MTP-131 by Intravenous Infusion in Rats (Study SPI-CRL-14-04; GLP: Yes)
L1399: Key Study Findings
L1400: • Decreased body weight gain (15%) was observed at high dose during GD 10-12 when compared to the control. Body weight gain recovered later. No other maternal toxicity was observed up to the high dose of 10 mg/kg/day.
• No developmental toxicity was noted up to the highest dose tested.
• The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 3 mg/kg/day; the NOAEL for embryofetal developmental toxicity was 10 mg/kg/day.

L1407: ParameterMajor Findings



MortalityNo test article related mortality was observed.

Clinical signsNo significant clinical signs of toxicity were noted.

Body weightsDecreased body weight gain during GD 7 to GD 10 (9%) and during GD 10 to 12 (15%) was observed at high dose. No significant body weight gain reduction in the subsequent dosing days.

Reproductive PerformanceNo differences in the litter average, corpora lutea, implantations, the percentage of pre- or postimplantation loss, litter sizes, live fetuses, placental morphology, fetal body weights, and the percentage of live male fetuses between treated and control groups was noted.

Necropsy Findings, Cesarean Section DataNo maternal gross findings were observed at necropsy.

Necropsy Findings OffspringIncidence of microphthalmia retinal fold (1 fetus), slight dilation of the brain ventricles (1 fetus), and moderate dilation of the ureters (1 fetus) in the high dose and microphthalmia (1 fetus, 3 mg/kg/day) were noted. These findings were not dose-dependent, and the incidence of the findings were within laboratory historical controls. No dose-dependent changes in external, soft tissue or skeletal abnormalities in the fetuses were noted.

L1414: Rabbit Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study
L1415: Preliminary Dose Range Finding Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study in Rabbits (Study SPI-CRL-14-01; GLP: No)
L1416: A preliminary, dose range-finding, EFD toxicity study was conducted in timed-pregnant New Zealand White rabbits (n=2) in two phases. In the first phase, elamipretide was intravenously administered [gestation day (GD) 7 to GD 19] at doses of 0, 2.5 and 25 mg/kg/hr for 4 hours (total dose: 0, 10 and 100 mg/kg/day) and in the second phase, at doses of 0, 6.25 and 12.5 mg/kg/hr for 4 hours (total dose: 0, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day). High dose (100 mg/kg/day) caused death of one animal due to clinical signs of toxicity. In the surviving high dose animals, tachypnea, ptosis, scant feces, reduced feed consumption and weight loss was noted between GD 7 and GD 12. Lower doses (10, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day) did not cause maternal or developmental toxicity. The high dose of 50 mg/kg/day via IV infusion for the definitive rabbit EFD study was considered reasonable given severe toxicities observed at the 100 mg/kg/day dose.
L1417: MTP-131: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study by Intravenous Infusion in Rabbits (Study SPI-CRL-14-02; GLP: Yes)
L1418: Key Study Findings
L1419: • No dose-dependent effects were seen on body weights and body weight gains. Decrease in body weights (40 to 60%) and food consumption (85%) were noted in mid and high dose groups.
• No test article-dependent changes were noted in the: (1) corpora lutea, implantation sites, embryo-fetal viability, fetal body weights and fetal sex; (2) external or visceral fetal malformations of the fetus; and (3) fetal skeletal system.
• Elamipretide (15 and 50 mg/kg/day) caused transient reduction in body weights and feed consumption. No significant developmental effects were seen up to the highest dose tested.
